\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}=\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}\frac{1}{\left[\prod_{j=1}^{3}2|\vec{y}-\vec{x}_{j}|\right]\prod_{\ell=2}^{3}(|\vec{x}_{\ell}-\vec{y}|+|\vec{y}-\vec{x}_{1}|-|\vec{x}_{\ell}-\vec{x}_{1}|)}.

(82)

According to eq. (
80
), there is a pinched singularity when
\vec{y}^{\,\star}=\vec{x}_{3}+\alpha(\vec{x}_{1}-\vec{x}_{3})
,
\alpha\in[0,1]
(and analogously when substituting
2
with
3
). Using the parameterization
\vec{y}^{\,\star}=\vec{x}_{3}+\alpha(\vec{x}_{1}-\vec{x}_{3})+\vec{y}^{\perp}
, we see that the measure contributes with two powers of the transverse variable
\vec{y}^{\perp}
, while eq. (
81
) makes it clear that the denominator also contributes with two powers. Overall, this gives a logarithmically divergent behaviour in this limit.
This finding is odd: in momentum space, such a diagram would be completely finite and, for a cubic scalar theory, it would (after truncation of external propagators) amount to a constant.

2.

The triangle
: We look specifically at the orientation

x_{1}

x_{2}

x_{3}

y_{1}

y_{2}

y_{3}

(83)

and at the pinched singular surface identified by the implicit equation

\gamma_{\textrm{r}}-|\vec{x}_{1}-\vec{x}_{2}|=|\vec{x}_{2}-\vec{y}_{2}|+|\vec{y}_{2}-\vec{y}_{1}|+|\vec{y}_{1}-\vec{x}_{1}|-|\vec{x}_{1}-\vec{x}_{2}|=0,

which has the solution

\vec{y}_{1}^{\,\star}(\alpha_{1})=\vec{x}_{1}+\alpha_{1}(\vec{x}_{2}-\vec{x}_{1}),\quad\vec{y}_{2}^{\,\star}(\alpha_{2})=\vec{x}_{1}+\alpha_{2}(\vec{x}_{2}-\vec{x}_{1}),\quad\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in[0,1],\,\,\alpha_{2}>\alpha_{1}.

In order to approach the limit, we consider the parameterization
\vec{y}_{1}=\vec{y}_{1}^{\,\star}(\alpha_{1})+\vec{y}^{\perp}_{1}
,
\vec{y}_{2}=\vec{y}_{2}^{\,\star}(\alpha_{2})+\vec{y}^{\perp}_{2}
. A simple power-counting procedure leads us to conclude that the integration measure scales as
\mathrm{d}^{2}\vec{y}_{2}^{\perp}\mathrm{d}^{2}\vec{y}_{1}^{\perp}
while the singular surface only gives a contribution of two powers in the denominator. Overall, this gives an integrable degree of divergence. Carrying out the same argument for all other pinched surfaces of all triangle orientations as well as the intersections of pinched surfaces, one finds that the triangle has no finite distance singularity
4
4

4

In contrast, in ref.
ErdoganCS
, a logarithmic finite distance singularity is found for the triangle. However, in their derivation, one of the three internal vertices is set to be at the origin, and the corresponding external leg is truncated.
.

The fact that these two diagrams do not reproduce the expectations concerning their IR structure invites us to change our perspective, and shift our attention to the S-matrix. First, we note that the S-matrix is constructed from truncated Green’s functions, while here we considered FOPT expressions for non-truncated Green’s functions. Indeed this is the reason that the hen’s claw has singular denominators in the first place. On top of this, we must set
x_{i(\textrm{r})}^{0}-x_{f(\textrm{r})}^{0}=|\vec{x}_{f(\textrm{r})}-\vec{x}_{i(\textrm{r})}|
, which is at odds with localization of momenta on the mass-shell. These two observations lead us to examine the S-matrix to correctly identify IR singularities in the FOPT formalism. The S-matrix is a truncated object and involves integration over the external coordinates
x_{a}^{0}
, for all
a\in V^{\text{ext}}
, via the Fourier transform to momentum space.

3.2
Long distance singularities

We now briefly discuss long distance singularities of FOPT orientations, which we would expect to map one-to-one to their momentum space analogues. In order to do so, let us look again at the orientation of eq. (
83
) in the triangle diagram, and comment on the absence of such long distance singularities. In particular, consider the following limits

1.

Single limits: the limit
\vec{y}_{1}\rightarrow\infty
has the measure scaling as
|\vec{y}_{1}|^{2}\mathrm{d}\vec{y}_{1}
and the denominator scaling as
|\vec{y}_{1}|^{6}
, giving integrable behaviour. The limit
\vec{y}_{2}\rightarrow\infty
has the measure scaling as
|\vec{y}_{2}|^{2}\mathrm{d}\vec{y}_{2}
and the denominator scaling as
|\vec{y}_{1}|^{5}
, giving integrable behaviour. Finally, the limit
\vec{y}_{3}\rightarrow\infty
has the measure scaling like
|\vec{y}_{3}|^{2}\mathrm{d}\vec{y}_{3}
and the denominator scaling as
|\vec{y}_{1}|^{5}
, giving integrable behaviour.

2.

Double limits: consider taking the limit
\vec{y}_{1},\vec{y}_{2}\rightarrow\infty
. The scaling is independent from
\vec{y}_{1}
and
\vec{y}_{2}
being collinear, as
|\vec{y}_{1}-\vec{y}_{2}|
is finite in the collinear case. But the angle between
\vec{y}_{1}
and
\vec{y}_{2}
has to be fixed too, giving an extra suppressing power from the integration measure. Thus, let us study the non-collinear case: we have the measure scaling as
|\vec{y}_{1}|^{2}|\vec{y}_{2}|^{2}\mathrm{d}\vec{y}_{1}\mathrm{d}\vec{y}_{2}
, while the denominator scales as
|\vec{y}_{1}-\vec{y}_{2}||\vec{y}_{1}|^{3}|\vec{y}_{2}|^{2}\text{max}(|\vec{y}_{1}|^{2},|\vec{y}_{2}|^{2})
, giving integrable behaviour. Analogous integrable behaviour can be found in the remaining two double limits.

The only limit that yields non-integrable behaviour is the one in which all three vertices of the triangle approach infinity in different directions, which has no clear analogue in momentum space.

The observations pointed out above motivate changing focus to the S-matrix in order to correctly analyze IR singularities in FOPT diagrams. We will do this by using the FOPT representation from the last section to construct a
hybrid
representation for the S-matrix, where external parameters are given in momentum space, while internal integrations are performed in coordinate space.

4
The S-matrix and its
p
-
x
representation

The FOPT representation, derived in sect.
2.3
, gives a
canonical representation of QFT correlation functions in terms of three-dimensional integrals. However, its external data is given in coordinate space, which makes it ill-suited for the direct computation of realistic collider observables. This is reflected in the absence of a clear counterpart to IR singularities in the FOPT representation, which in turn has repercussions on how manifestly physical principles such as parton degeneracy and collinear mass factorization are realised within it.

In this section we will address this problem by constructing a new perturbative representation of the
S-matrix
in momentum space. It is expressed as an integral over spatial coordinates, even though the external data is given in momentum space. We therefore call it the
p
-
x
representation of the S-matrix, as a reference to its hybrid nature.

Recall that the retarded and advanced propagators in momentum space can be written as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta}_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{3}{R}{A}}(p)=\frac{i}{(p_{0}\pm i\varepsilon)^{2}-\vec{p}^{2}}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\textrm{d}^{4}ze^{iz\cdot p}\delta^{(\pm)}(z^{2})=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\textrm{d}^{3}\vec{z}}{2|\vec{z}\,|}e^{\pm i|\vec{z}|p^{0}-i\vec{z}\cdot\vec{p}}\;.

(84)

To give an expression for the S-matrix, we have to fix in- and out-going momenta. In anticipation of our FOPT treatment of the S-matrix we index the in-going momenta by
V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}
,
\{p_{i}\}_{i\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}}
and the out-going momenta by
V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{out}}
,
\{p_{f}\}_{f\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{out}}}
.
The S-matrix element in terms of the in- and out-going momenta is given by

S(\{p_{i}\}_{i\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}},\{p_{f}\}_{f\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{out}}})=Z^{|V_{\text{ext}}|/2}\widetilde{\Gamma}_{T}(\{p_{a}\}_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}})\,,

(85)

where
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{T}
is the truncated Green’s function, with the typical convention that energy directed towards the graph is positive, such that

p_{i}^{0}>0
and
p_{f}^{0}<0
, and
Z^{|V_{\text{ext}}|/2}
is the factor accounting for the truncation of external self-energies, as established by the LSZ formula.
The external momenta should be set on-shell eventually, i.e.
p_{i}^{0}\rightarrow|\vec{p}_{i}|
, for
i\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}
and
p_{f}^{0}\rightarrow-|\vec{p}_{f}|
, for
f\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{out}}
.
The truncated Green’s function can be obtained from the ordinary Green’s function with no external self-energy insertions by the rule

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{T}(\{p_{a}\}_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}})=\Bigg[\prod_{i\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}}\widetilde{\Delta}_{R}(p_{i})\Bigg]^{-1}\Bigg[\prod_{f\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{out}}}\widetilde{\Delta}_{A}(p_{f})\Bigg]^{-1}\widetilde{\Gamma}(\{p_{a}\}_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}}).

(86)

Notice that we use advanced and retarded propagators for in- and out-going particles respectively, instead of the Feynman propagator
\widetilde{\Delta}_{F}
. This is justified by the simple equation
\widetilde{\Delta}_{F}(p)=\Theta(p^{0})\widetilde{\Delta}_{R}(p)+\Theta(-p^{0})\widetilde{\Delta}_{A}(p)
and the fact that in-coming (out-going) particles are taken to have positive (negative) energy
p^{0}
.
We stress that
\widetilde{\Gamma}
has no external self-energy insertions, but it does have external propagators.
\widetilde{\Gamma}
can then be obtained by Fourier transforming its coordinate space counterpart, which we can express using FOPT. In particular, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Gamma}(\{p_{a}\}_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}})=\int\left[\prod_{a\in V^{\text{ext}}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x_{a}\,e^{ix_{a}\cdot p_{a}}\right]\Gamma(\{x_{a}\}_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}}),

(87)

where
\Gamma(\{x_{a}\}_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}})
is the coordinate space correlation function in eq. (
3
) and, in agreement to the combinatorial notation of sect.
2.6
,
V^{\textrm{ext}}=V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}\cup V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{out}}
.

4.1
Derivation of the
p
-
x
representation

In this section we derive the
p
-
x
representation of the S-matrix.
It is a representation of eq. (
86
) in terms of coordinate space integrals
that are modulated by the Fourier transformation of a certain polytope.
We will show that this polytope is a variant of the
flow polytope
(see e.g.
baldoni2008kostant
).

We use the FOPT representation of the correlation function in eq. (
50
) to express the Fourier transformed correlation function in eq. (
87
):

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Gamma}(\{p_{a}\}_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}})=\sum_{(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})}\frac{1}{\Sym(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})}\widetilde{A}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{a}\}_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}}),

(88)

where the Fourier transform of a FOPT orientation is given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{a}\}_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}})=\int\left[\prod_{a\in V^{\text{ext}}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x_{a}\,e^{ix_{a}\cdot p_{a}}\right]A_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{x_{a}\}_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}}).

(89)

We can use the FOPT representation with explicit separation of external and internal paths, i.e. cycles and routes, in eq. (
2.6
) to write
\widetilde{A}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{a}\}_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}})
as

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\widetilde{A}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{a}\}_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}})=\\
\frac{(2\pi g)^{|V^{\textrm{int}}|}}{(-4\pi^{2})^{|E|}}\int\frac{\left(\prod_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{x}_{a}e^{-i\vec{x}_{a}\cdot\vec{p}_{a}}\right)\left(\prod_{v\in V^{\text{int}}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{v}\right)}{\left(\prod_{e\in E}2|\vec{z}_{e}|\right)\left(\prod_{\textrm{c}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{int}}}}\gamma_{\textrm{c}}\right)}f_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{a}^{0}\}_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}},\{\gamma_{\textrm{r}}\}_{\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}}),\end{gathered}

where we split off the nontrivial time integration, which we plan on performing analytically, through the definition

\displaystyle f_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{a}^{0}\}_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}},\{\gamma_{\textrm{r}}\}_{\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}})=\int\frac{\prod_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}}\mathrm{d}x_{a}^{0}e^{ix_{a}^{0}p_{a}^{0}}}{\prod_{\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}}(\gamma_{\textrm{r}}-x_{i(\textrm{r})}^{0}+x_{f(\textrm{r})}^{0}+i\varepsilon)}.

(92)

Let us perform the energy integrations over the external time variables
x_{a}^{0}
analytically. To this end we follow steps analogous to those performed in the derivation of sect.
2.3
.
We introduce one auxiliary integration variable
z_{\textrm{r}}^{0}
for each route
r
, parameterizing the time difference between the external vertices
i(\textrm{r})
and
f(\textrm{r})
,

f_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=\int\left[\prod_{a\in V^{\text{ext}}}\mathrm{d}x_{a}^{0}\,e^{ix_{a}^{0}p_{a}^{0}}\right]\prod_{\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}}\mathrm{d}z_{\textrm{r}}^{0}\frac{\delta(z_{\textrm{r}}^{0}-x_{i(\textrm{r})}^{0}+x_{f(\textrm{r})}^{0})}{\gamma_{\textrm{r}}-z_{\textrm{r}}^{0}+i\varepsilon}.

(93)

We then rewrite the delta functions using their Fourier representation, thus introducing an extra integration in the energy
E_{\textrm{r}}
,

f_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=\int\left[\prod_{a\in V^{\text{ext}}}\mathrm{d}x_{a}^{0}\,e^{ix_{a}^{0}p_{a}^{0}}\right]\prod_{\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}z_{\textrm{r}}^{0}\mathrm{d}E_{\textrm{r}}}{2\pi}\frac{e^{iE_{\textrm{r}}\left(z_{\textrm{r}}^{0}-x_{i(\textrm{r})}^{0}+x_{f(\textrm{r})}^{0}\right)}}{\gamma_{\textrm{r}}-z_{\textrm{r}}^{0}+i\varepsilon}.

(94)

Integration over
z_{\textrm{r}}^{0}
is performed trivially using the residue theorem. For
E_{\textrm{r}}>0
, we close the contour in the upper-half of complex plane, where the pole
z_{\textrm{r}}^{0}=\gamma_{\textrm{r}}+i\varepsilon
is located. For
E_{\textrm{r}}<0
, we close the contour in the lower-half of complex plane, where no pole is located. In summary, this gives

f_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=(-i)^{|{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}|}\int\left[\prod_{a\in V^{\text{ext}}}\mathrm{d}x_{a}^{0}\,e^{ix_{a}^{0}p_{a}^{0}}\right]\prod_{\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}}\mathrm{d}E_{\textrm{r}}e^{iE_{\textrm{r}}\left(\gamma_{\textrm{r}}-x_{i(\textrm{r})}^{0}+x_{f(\textrm{r})}^{0}+i\varepsilon\right)}\Theta(E_{\textrm{r}}).

(95)

To perform the integration over
x_{a}^{0}
, we may write

\displaystyle f_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=(-i)^{|{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}|}\int\prod_{\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}}\left[\mathrm{d}E_{\textrm{r}}e^{iE_{\textrm{r}}(\gamma_{\textrm{r}}+i\varepsilon)}\Theta(E_{\textrm{r}})\right]

\displaystyle\times\left[\prod_{i\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}}\int\mathrm{d}x_{i}^{0}\,\exp\left({ix_{i}^{0}p_{i}^{0}-i\sum_{\textrm{r}\ni i}E_{\textrm{r}}x_{i}^{0}}\right)\right]\left[\prod_{f\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{out}}}\int\mathrm{d}x_{f}^{0}\,\exp\left({ix_{f}^{0}p_{f}^{0}+i\sum_{\textrm{r}\ni f}E_{\textrm{r}}x_{f}^{0}}\right)\right],

(96)

where we sum over all routes that include the indicated in- or out-going vertex in the last two exponentials.
Using the Fourier representation of the
\delta
function, we can resolve the integrals over the
x_{a}^{0}
variables, yielding

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}f_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=\frac{(-i)^{|{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}|}}{(2\pi)^{|V^{\textrm{ext}}|}}\int\left[\prod_{\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}}\mathrm{d}E_{\textrm{r}}e^{iE_{\textrm{r}}(\gamma_{\textrm{r}}+i\varepsilon)}\Theta(E_{\textrm{r}})\right]\\
\times\left[\prod_{i\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}}\delta\left(p_{i}^{0}-\sum_{\textrm{r}\ni i}E_{\textrm{r}}\right)\right]\left[\prod_{f\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{out}}}\delta\left(p_{f}^{0}+\sum_{\textrm{r}\ni f}E_{\textrm{r}}\right)\right].\end{gathered}

The
\Theta
and
\delta
functions in the above integral describe a polytope in the
|{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}|
dimensional space of the energy integrations. This polytope is a variant of the
flow polytope
which has various applications in combinatorics.
We conclude that
f_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
is an evaluation of the
Fourier transform
of this polytope, as we integrate
\exp\left(i\sum_{\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}}E_{\textrm{r}}(\gamma_{\textrm{r}}+i\varepsilon)\right)
.
The full form of the
p
-
x
S-matrix representation is most conveniently given using this polytope.
For this reason, we will describe this polytope in detail in the next section.

4.2
The flow polytope

To every digraph
(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})
with a given set of in- and out-going energies
\{p^{0}_{i}\}_{i\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}}
and
\{p^{0}_{f}\}_{f\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{out}}}
,
we associate a polytope,

\mathcal{F}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{|{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}|}
.
The polytope is defined by linear equalities and inequalities that are given in the energy coordinates
E_{\textrm{r}}
associated to the routes
\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}
. For each path, the respective energy represents the amount of energy that flows through the specified path. Accordingly, the delta functions in eq. (
4.1
) enforce energy conservation at each external vertex. In summary,
\mathcal{F}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}

is swept out by all tuples
(E_{\textrm{r}})_{\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}}
which fulfill

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} E_{\textrm{r}}&\geq 0\text{ for all }\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}\,,\\
\sum_{\textrm{r}\ni i}E_{\textrm{r}}&=\phantom{-}p^{0}_{i}\text{ for all }i\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}},\\
\sum_{\textrm{r}\ni f}E_{\textrm{r}}&=-p^{0}_{f}\text{ for all }f\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{out}}.\end{aligned}

(99)

The inequalities ensure a positive energy flow through each route and the two sets of equalities ensure energy conservation at in- and out-going external vertices. Note that the equations above imply overall energy conservation for the external data: summing all the equalities with
i\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}
and subtracting those with
f\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{out}}
, one obtains that
\sum_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}}p^{0}_{a}=0
, since each path has exactly one in- and one out-going vertex.

The eqs. (
99
) define a convex polytope, obtained from the intersection of the positive orthant
\{(E_{\textrm{r}})_{\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}|}:E_{\textrm{r}}\geq 0\}
with the
|V^{\textrm{ext}}|
hyperplanes given by energy conservation constraints at the external vertices. It is moreover bounded, since the energies are contained in the hypercube,
0\leq E_{\textrm{r}}\leq\min\left(p_{i(\textrm{r})}^{0},-p_{f(\textrm{r})}^{0}\right)
.
We can also find, depending on the value of the external data, configurations of external energies where the eqs. (
99
) cannot be fulfilled. In those cases, the flow polytope has no support and the contribution of the digraph
(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})
to the S-matrix is zero.

To simplify the notation, in the following we denote vectors in the route space
\mathbb{R}^{|{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}|}
with bold letters. The tuples
(E_{\textrm{r}})_{\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}|}
are denoted as
\boldsymbol{E}\in\mathbb{R}^{|{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}|}
and tuples of path lengths
(\gamma_{\textrm{r}})_{\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}|}
as
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}^{|{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}|}
. We also can define the standard scalar product
\boldsymbol{E}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\sum_{\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}}E_{\textrm{r}}\gamma_{\textrm{r}}
.
We may use the definition of the polytope and the boldface notation to rewrite eq. (
4.1
) in a more compact form,

\displaystyle f_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{a}^{0}\}_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}},\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\frac{(-i)^{|{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}|}}{(2\pi)^{|V^{\textrm{ext}}|}}\delta\left(\sum_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}}p^{0}_{a}\right)\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}+i\varepsilon\boldsymbol{1})

(100)

where
\boldsymbol{1}=(1,\ldots,1)
is the all-ones-vector in
\mathbb{R}^{|{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}|}
.
The
Fourier transform
,
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})

of the polytope
\mathcal{F}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}
is given by

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}+i\varepsilon\boldsymbol{1})=\int_{\mathcal{F}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}}\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{E}\,e^{i\boldsymbol{E}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\gamma}+i\varepsilon\boldsymbol{1})}.

(101)

The integration over the bounded domain
\mathcal{F}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}
,
allows us to drop the
i\varepsilon
prescription in the generic case.
We will however keep it in place now to study singular configurations later on.
The Fourier transform of a polytope enjoys many remarkable properties.
For instance it is bounded by the volume of the polytope. In our case we have

\left|\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}+i\varepsilon\boldsymbol{1})\right|\leq\mathrm{Vol}\left(\mathcal{F}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}\right)\text{ for all }\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}^{|{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}|}.

(102)

Equality is only attained for
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=0
.
Fourier transforms of polytopes are always linear combinations of rational functions with phase factors as coefficients. An analytic expression for such Fourier transforms can always be obtained algorithmically by computing a
facet presentation
of the associated polytope (see e.g.
polytope_fourier
).

4.3
Truncated routes

In this section we will relate FOPT integrals to
truncated FOPT integrals
.
This relation is a special case of a general
factorization
property of
the Fourier transform of the flow polytope.

x_{1}

x_{5}

x_{2}

x_{3}

x_{4}

r

\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.5625,1}\textrm{r}^{\prime}

(a)

Two routes
r
and
\textrm{r}^{\prime}

y_{\overline{1}}

y_{\overline{2}}

y_{\overline{3}}

y_{\overline{5}}

y_{\overline{4}}

\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\textrm{r}^{\mathrm{t}}

\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.5625,1}(\textrm{r}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{t}}

(b)

Respective truncated routes

Figure 3
:
Illustration of the truncation of routes.

Let
r
be a route joining the external vertices
i(\textrm{r})\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}
and
f(\textrm{r})\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{out}}
. Furthermore, let
\overline{i(\textrm{r})}
and
\overline{f(\textrm{r})}
denote the only vertices adjacent to
i(\textrm{r})
and
f(\textrm{r})
, respectively. The truncated version of the route
r
is given by all edges of
r
except for the initial and final one. We will denote the length of the truncated route of a route
r
as
\gamma_{\textrm{r}}^{\textrm{t}}
(see Figure
3
). The relation between the length
\gamma_{\textrm{r}}
of the original route and the truncated route is

\gamma_{\textrm{r}}=\gamma_{\textrm{r}}^{\mathrm{t}}+|\vec{x}_{i(\textrm{r})}-\vec{y}_{\,\overline{i(\textrm{r})}}|+|\vec{x}_{f(\textrm{r})}-\vec{y}_{\,\overline{f(\textrm{r})}}|.

(103)

It also follows that the length of the truncated route,
\gamma_{\textrm{r}}^{\textrm{t}}
, does not depend on the position of the external vertices
\vec{x}_{a}
,
a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}
.
Using this new notation we get, for scalar products in route space,

\boldsymbol{E}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\sum_{\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}}E_{\textrm{r}}\gamma_{\textrm{r}}=\sum_{\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}}E_{\textrm{r}}\left(|\vec{x}_{i(\textrm{r})}-\vec{y}_{\,\overline{i(\textrm{r})}}|+|\vec{x}_{f(\textrm{r})}-\vec{y}_{\,\overline{f(\textrm{r})}}|\right)+\sum_{\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}}E_{\textrm{r}}\gamma_{\textrm{r}}^{\mathrm{t}}.

(104)

We can require that
\boldsymbol{E}\in\mathcal{F}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}

and use the constraints in eqs. (
4.1
) to get

\sum_{\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}}E_{\textrm{r}}|\vec{x}_{i(\textrm{r})}-\vec{y}_{\,\overline{i(\textrm{r})}}|=\sum_{i\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}}|\vec{x}_{i}-\vec{y}_{\,\overline{i}}|\sum_{\textrm{r}\ni i}E_{\textrm{r}}=\sum_{i\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}}p_{i}^{0}\,|\vec{x}_{i}-\vec{y}_{\,\overline{i}}|,

(105)

and similarly,

\sum_{\textrm{r}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}}E_{\textrm{r}}|\vec{x}_{f(\textrm{r})}-\vec{y}_{\,\overline{f(\textrm{r})}}|=-\sum_{f\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}}p_{f}^{0}\,|\vec{x}_{f}-\vec{y}_{\,\overline{f}}|.
Hence,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\sum_{i\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}}p_{i}^{0}\,|\vec{x}_{i}-\vec{y}_{\,\overline{i}}|-\sum_{f\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}}p_{f}^{0}\,|\vec{x}_{f}-\vec{y}_{\,\overline{f}}|+\boldsymbol{E}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\textrm{t}}\,.

(106)

Applying these observations to eq. (
100
)
and using the definition of the Fourier transform of the flow polytope in eq. (
101
) gives

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}f_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=\frac{(-i)^{|{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}|}}{(2\pi)^{|V^{\textrm{ext}}|}}\delta\left(\sum_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}}p^{0}_{a}\right)\left[\prod_{i\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}}e^{ip_{i}^{0}|\vec{x}_{i}-\vec{y}_{\,\overline{i}}|}\right]\left[\prod_{f\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{out}}}e^{-ip_{f}^{0}|\vec{x}_{f}-\vec{y}_{\,\overline{f}}|}\right]\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\textrm{t}}).\end{gathered}

We can now use the definition of the retarded and advanced propagators in eq. (
84
) to relate the oscillating exponentials that we manifestly extracted from the Fourier transform of the polytope to advanced and retarded propagators. In particular, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{x}_{a}}{2|\vec{x}_{a}-\vec{y}_{\,\overline{a}}|}e^{\pm i{p_{a}^{0}}|\vec{x}_{a}-\vec{y}_{\,\overline{a}}|-i\vec{x}_{a}\cdot\vec{p}_{a}}=e^{-i\vec{y}_{\,\overline{a}}\cdot\vec{p}_{a}}\widetilde{\Delta}_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{3}{R}{A}}(p).

(108)

Substituting eq. (
4.3
) into eq. (
4.1
), and using eq. (
108
), results in

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{a}\})=\frac{(2\pi g)^{|V^{\textrm{int}}|}i^{|V^{\textrm{ext}}|}}{(-4\pi^{2})^{|E|}i^{|{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}|}}\Bigg[\prod_{i\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}}\widetilde{\Delta}_{R}(p_{i})\Bigg]\Bigg[\prod_{f\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{out}}}\widetilde{\Delta}_{A}(p_{f})\Bigg]\delta\left(\sum_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}}p^{0}_{a}\right)

\displaystyle\times\int\frac{\left[\prod_{v\in V^{\text{int}}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{v}\right]\left[\prod_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}}e^{-i\vec{y}_{\,\overline{a}}\cdot\vec{p}_{a}}\right]}{\left[\prod_{e\in E^{\textrm{int}}}2|\vec{z}_{e}|\right]\left[\prod_{\textrm{c}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{int}}}}\gamma_{\textrm{c}}\right]}\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\textrm{t}}+i\varepsilon\boldsymbol{1}),

(109)

where
E^{\textrm{int}}
is the set of internal edges of
(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})
.

Similar factorization of the Fourier transform of the flow polytope arises when the underlying graph
has a separating edge, i.e. an edge whose removal disconnects the graph. It can be checked that in these cases, the Fourier transform of the whole graph factors into the Fourier transforms of the flow polytopes associated to the 1PI components of the graph and a couple of trivial phase factors. For instance, for a tree-level graph, the conditions in eq. (
99
) have at most one solution. This solution is uniquely determined by enforcing energy conservation at all vertices of the tree. Hence, the associated Fourier transform then consists exclusively of phase factors.

4.4
The
p
-
x
S-matrix representation

The
p
-
x
representation of the S-matrix follows by
comparing eq. (
4.3
) with the FOPT expansion of the correlation function in eq. (
88
) and the definition of the S-matrix in eq. (
86
):

S(\{p_{i}\}_{i\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}},\{p_{f}\}_{f\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{out}}})=\sum_{(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})}\frac{1}{\Sym(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})}S_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{i}\}_{i\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}},\{p_{f}\}_{f\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{out}}}),

(110)

where we sum over all FOPT graphs. Following the partition of in- and out-going external vertices as indicated by the in- and out-going energies
\{p_{a}^{0}\}
, the S-matrix element
S_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{i}\}_{i\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}},\{p_{f}\}_{f\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{out}}})
is given by

\displaystyle S_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=

\displaystyle\frac{Z^{|V^{\text{ext}}|/2}(2\pi g)^{|V^{\textrm{int}}|}i^{|V^{\textrm{ext}}|}}{(-4\pi^{2})^{|E|}i^{|{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}|}}\delta\left(\sum_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}}p^{0}_{a}\right)

\displaystyle\times\int\frac{\left[\prod_{v\in V^{\text{int}}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{v}\right]\left[\prod_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}}e^{-i\vec{y}_{\,\overline{a}}\cdot\vec{p}_{a}}\right]}{\left[\prod_{e\in E^{\textrm{int}}}2|\vec{z}_{e}|\right]\left[\prod_{\textrm{c}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{int}}}}\gamma_{\textrm{c}}\right]}\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\textrm{t}}+i\varepsilon\boldsymbol{1}).

(111)

Except for the oscillating exponential term, the integral above is invariant under joint translations of all internal coordinates. We may pick some root vertex
w\in V^{\textrm{int}}
of the graph and shift all other
\vec{y}
-vectors as
\vec{y}_{v}\rightarrow\vec{y}_{w}+\vec{y}_{v}
. This way, we remove all nontrivial dependence of the
\vec{y}_{w}
vector from the integrand. The integration over
\vec{y}_{w}
gives a momentum-conservation-ensuring
\delta
function. In equations,

\displaystyle S_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=\frac{Z^{|V^{\text{ext}}|/2}(2\pi)^{3}(2\pi g)^{|V^{\textrm{int}}|}i^{|V^{\textrm{ext}}|}}{(-4\pi^{2})^{|E|}i^{|{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}|}}\delta^{(4)}\left(\sum_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}}p_{a}\right)s_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{i}\}_{i\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}},\{p_{f}\}_{f\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{out}}}),

(112)

where
s_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=(\{p_{i}\}_{i\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}},\{p_{f}\}_{f\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{out}}})
is the
reduced
S-matrix element without trivial prefactors

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}s_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=\int\left[\frac{\prod_{v\in V^{\text{int}}\setminus\{w\}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{v}}{\prod_{e\in E^{\textrm{int}}}2|\vec{z}_{e}|}\right]\left[\frac{\prod_{a\in V^{\textrm{ext}}}e^{-i\vec{y}_{\,\overline{a}}\cdot\vec{p}_{a}}}{\left[\prod_{\textrm{c}\in{\Gamma^{\textrm{int}}}}\gamma_{\textrm{c}}\right]}\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\textrm{t}}+i\varepsilon\boldsymbol{1})\right]\Bigg|_{\vec{y}_{w}=0}.\end{gathered}

Note that this formula is, up to the contribution of the cycles, a 3-dimensional coordinate space Feynman integral
that is modulated by a Fourier transformation of the flow polytope.
We stress that this is a
hybrid
representation for the S-matrix contributions: the external kinematics are given in momentum space, but internal integrations are performed in coordinate space. We will call eq. (
112
) the
p
-
x
representation of the S-matrix
in order to emphasise this fact. This hybrid aspect is crucial for our investigation of collinear and soft singularities.

4.5
Example: The
p
-
x
representation of a triangle diagram

To illustrate the
p
-
x
representation of the S-matrix we discuss here the contribution to the S-matrix from our recurring example, the oriented triangle diagram

}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 98.43 -59.06 M 102.36 -59.06 C 102.36 -56.88 100.6 -55.12 98.43 -55.12 C 96.25 -55.12 94.49 -56.88 94.49 -59.06 C 94.49 -61.23 96.25 -62.99 98.43 -62.99 C 100.6 -62.99 102.36 -61.23 102.36 -59.06 Z M 98.43 -59.06}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}} },

(114)

where we now label the external vertex
x_{i}
with its Fourier conjugate momentum
p_{i}
. In accordance with the all-in-going momentum convention we require that
p_{1}^{0}>0
and
p_{2}^{0},p_{3}^{0}<0
. The routes of this digraph have been illustrated in eq. (
2.2
). It has the three truncated routes
\textrm{r}_{1}^{\textrm{t}}=\{e_{4}\}
,
\textrm{r}_{2}^{\textrm{t}}=\{e_{5}\}
and
\textrm{r}_{3}^{\textrm{t}}=\{e_{5},e_{6}\}
. Hence,
\gamma^{\textrm{t}}_{1}=|\vec{z}_{4}|,\gamma^{\textrm{t}}_{2}=|\vec{z}_{5}|,\gamma^{\textrm{t}}_{3}=|\vec{z}_{5}|+|\vec{z}_{6}|
. Let
E_{1},E_{2}
and
E_{3}
be the energies that flow through the respective route.

The flow polytope
\mathcal{F}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}
for this digraph is cut out by the conditions,

\displaystyle\begin{split}E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}&\geq 0\,,\\
E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3}&=p_{1}^{0}\,,\\
E_{1}+E_{3}&=-p_{2}^{0}\,,\\
E_{2}&=-p_{3}^{0}\,,\end{split}

(115)

where one of the last three equations is redundant by overall momentum conservation, which is assumed. We can give an interpretation to the energy-conservation condition of eq. (
115
) as follows: for each external vertex
v\in V^{\text{ext}}
, enumerate the paths that start or end at that vertex, and correspondingly sum their energies. Then, set the sum of such energies to be
p_{v}^{0}
if the vertex is the starting vertex for such paths or
-p_{v}^{0}
if it is an ending vertex. For the triangle example, we can represent such constraints graphically as follows:

E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3}=p_{1}^{0}
E_{1}+E_{3}=-p_{2}^{0}
E_{2}=-p_{3}^{0}

\color[rgb]{0.3906,0.5625,1}E_{1}

\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}E_{2}

\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}E_{3}

(116)

We can parameterize the polytope by
setting
(E_{1},E_{2},E_{3})=(E,-p_{3}^{0},-p_{2}^{0}-E)
and let
E
vary between
0
and
-p_{2}^{0}
. The polytope
\mathcal{F}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}
is therefore a 1-dimensional polytope, i.e. a line segment. Its volume is obviously
\textrm{Vol}(\mathcal{F}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}})=-p_{2}^{0}
(recall that
p_{2}^{0}<0
).
Using this parameterization, we can explicitly evaluate the Fourier transformation of the flow polytope associated to the digraph above,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\textrm{t}}+i\varepsilon\boldsymbol{1})=\int_{\mathcal{F}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}}\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{E}\,e^{i\boldsymbol{E}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\textrm{t}}+i\varepsilon\boldsymbol{1})}=\int_{0}^{-p_{2}^{0}}\textrm{d}E\,e^{iE(\gamma_{1}^{\textrm{t}}+i\varepsilon)-ip_{3}^{0}(\gamma_{2}^{\textrm{t}}+i\varepsilon)-i(p_{2}^{0}+E)(\gamma_{3}^{\textrm{t}}+i\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle=e^{-i(p_{3}^{0}+p_{2}^{0})(\gamma_{2}^{\textrm{t}}+i\varepsilon)}\int_{0}^{-p_{2}^{0}}\textrm{d}E\,e^{iE(\gamma_{1}^{\textrm{t}}-\gamma_{3}^{\textrm{t}})}=-ie^{-i(p_{3}^{0}+p_{2}^{0})(\gamma_{2}^{\textrm{t}}+i\varepsilon)}\frac{e^{-ip_{2}^{0}(\gamma_{1}^{\textrm{t}}-\gamma_{3}^{\textrm{t}})}-1}{\gamma_{1}^{\textrm{t}}-\gamma_{3}^{\textrm{t}}}

\displaystyle=-p_{2}^{0}e^{-ip_{3}^{0}(\gamma_{2}^{\textrm{t}}+i\varepsilon)-ip_{2}^{0}(\gamma_{2}^{\textrm{t}}+\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{1}^{\textrm{t}}-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{3}^{\textrm{t}}+i\varepsilon)}\sinc\left(\frac{p_{2}^{0}(\gamma_{1}^{\textrm{t}}-\gamma_{3}^{\textrm{t}})}{2}\right),

(117)

where
\sinc(x)=\frac{\sin(x)}{x}
. This expression is manifestly bounded as
\sinc(x)\leq 1
. The prefactor
-p_{2}^{0}
is the volume of the flow polytope as expected.
The reduced S-matrix contribution of the digraph above is,

\displaystyle s_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{1}\},\{p_{2},p_{3}\})=\int\frac{\left[\prod_{v\in\{2,3\}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{v}\right]\left[e^{-i\vec{y}_{2}\cdot\vec{p}_{2}-i\vec{y}_{3}\cdot\vec{p}_{3}}\right]}{8|\vec{z}_{4}||\vec{z}_{5}||\vec{z}_{6}|}\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\textrm{t}}+i\varepsilon\boldsymbol{1})\Big|_{\vec{y}_{1}=0},

(118)

where we used the freedom, guaranteed by translation invariance, to fix one vertex position at the origin, in this case
\vec{y}_{1}=0
.

4.6
Example: The flow polytope of a pentagon digraph

The previous example already illustrates that the only nontrivial part of the

p
-
x
S-matrix representation is the Fourier transform of the flow polytope. The Fourier transform of a polytope is a
perfect invariant
, i.e. it characterizes the polytope completely. Geometric understanding of the shape of this polytope is equivalent to knowledge of the associated Fourier transform.

It is a straightforward, algorithmic task to compute the shape of this polytope – even for rather intricate FOPT diagrams. To illustrate this point we compute the flow polytope of an oriented pentagon digraph:

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}} }.

(119)

The Fourier transform is parametric in the five external energies
p_{1}^{0},...,p_{5}^{0}
. We choose the energy signs such that they coincide with the FOPT orientation shown above, that is

p_{1}^{0},\,p_{2}^{0},\,p_{3}^{0}>0,\quad p_{4}^{0},\,p_{5}^{0}<0.

(120)

The six routes in
{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}
through the diagram are

\displaystyle\textrm{r}_{1}=\{e_{4},e_{6},e_{3}\},\quad

\displaystyle\textrm{r}_{2}=\{e_{4},e_{6},e_{9},e_{2}\},

\displaystyle\textrm{r}_{3}=\{e_{5},e_{8},e_{6},e_{3}\},\quad

\displaystyle\textrm{r}_{4}=\{e_{5},e_{8},e_{6},e_{9},e_{2}\},

(121)

\displaystyle\textrm{r}_{5}=\{e_{5},e_{7},e_{1}\},\quad

\displaystyle\textrm{r}_{6}=\{e_{5},e_{7},e_{10},e_{2}\},

and the Fourier transform of the flow polytope reads

\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\int_{\mathcal{F}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{E}\,e^{i\boldsymbol{\gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{E}}\,,

(122)

with
\mathcal{F}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}
being the polytope defined by the following constraints

\displaystyle E_{i}>0,\quad i=1,...,6

\displaystyle E_{1}+E_{3}=p_{3}^{0},\quad E_{5}=p_{1}^{0},\quad E_{2}+E_{4}+E_{6}=p_{2}^{0}\,,

(123)

\displaystyle E_{1}+E_{2}=-p_{4}^{0},\quad E_{3}+E_{4}+E_{5}+E_{6}=-p_{5}^{0}\,.

Due to overall momentum conservation,
one of the five equalities is linearly dependent on the others. The route space
\mathbb{R}^{|{\Gamma^{\textrm{ext}}}|}
is obviously six dimensional. Hence, the flow polytope is a two dimensional object embedded in six-dimensional space. To be able to draw it, we will project it on the plane spanned by the variables
E_{1}
and
E_{4}
. In this
E_{1}
-
E_{4}
-plane, the linear inequalities defining the positive orthant read

p_{3}^{0}-E_{1}>0,\quad-p_{4}^{0}-E_{1}>0,\quad p_{2}^{0}+p_{4}^{0}-E_{4}+E_{1}>0,\quad E_{1}>0,\quad E_{4}>0\,.

(124)

The domain identified by these linear inequalities is a bounded polytope in the
E_{1}
-
E_{4}
-plane, as expected, and is drawn as follows

\text{min}(p_{3}^{0},-p_{4}^{0})

p_{2}^{0}+p_{4}^{0}

p_{2}^{0}+p_{4}^{0}+\text{min}(p_{3}^{0},-p_{4}^{0})

E_{1}
E_{4}

The geometric properties of the polytope, such as the number of distinct edges and vertices, depend on the values of the external energies. If
p_{2}^{0}+p_{4}^{0}>0
, the polytope is a quadrilateral, a triangle if
-\text{min}(p_{3}^{0},-p_{4}^{0})<p_{2}^{0}+p_{4}^{0}\leq 0
, a point if
p_{2}^{0}+p_{4}^{0}+\text{min}(p_{3}^{0},-p_{4}^{0})=0
, and the empty set otherwise.

4.7
Polytopes and spurious singularities

The
p
-
x
S-matrix representation involves the Fourier transform of the flow polytope, an object that is both elegant and allows to use the existing mathematical literature on the subject to study its properties. One particular advantage is the absence of the spurious singularities that typically arise from contour integration of an integrand with multiple poles. A simple example of the appearance of spurious singularities is the following integral which depends on the parameters
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
,

\oint\mathrm{d}z\frac{f(z)}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}(z-a_{i})}=2\pi i\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{f(a_{i})}{\prod_{j\neq i}(a_{i}-a_{j})},

(125)

where the contour of integration shall contain all poles. The integral is defined whenever
a_{i}\neq a_{j}
for all
i\neq j
. One might expect the right-hand side of eq. (
125
) to be singular if two or more
a
-parameters coincide. However, if we make a subset of these parameters approach the same limit, i.e.
a_{i}\rightarrow a\in\mathbb{C}
, for all
i\in I\subseteq\{1,...,n\}
, then the right-hand side of eq. (
125
) remains finite as long as
f
is
|I|-1
times differentiable at
a
. For instance, in the case
n=2
, we have

\frac{f(a_{1})}{a_{1}-a_{2}}+\frac{f(a_{2})}{a_{2}-a_{1}}\rightarrow f^{\prime}(a),\quad\text{when }a_{1},a_{2}\rightarrow a,

(126)

showing that the singularity for
a_{1},a_{2}\rightarrow a
is spurious.

This simple model exemplifies the common occurrence of spurious cancellations in the study of IR singularities of amplitudes. Such cancellations are present in any method that is based on operating cuts or performing contour integration of Feynman integrals, including
dual cancellations
in the LTD formalism, and KLN cancellations
Kinoshita:1962ur
;
Lee:1964is
(see
Capatti_2020
for a study of KLN cancellations in terms of divided differences and
Hannesdottir:2022bmo
;
Frye:2018xjj
for an original approach on the KLN theorem) of IR singularities at the cross-section level. It is also not surprising that one of the motivating arguments in the amplituhedron approach
Arkani-Hamed:2012zlh
;
Arkani-Hamed:2013jha
;
Arkani-Hamed:2014dca
;
Hodges:2009hk
;
Hodges:2011wm
is its manifest realization of the cancellation of spurious singularities that are generated by the application of the BCFW recursion relation
Britto:2004ap
;
Britto:2005fq
. Spurious poles are also present in the
p
-
x
representation of the S-matrix, as exemplified by the
\sinc
-function in eq. (
117
), in the limit
\gamma_{\mathrm{r}_{1}}\rightarrow\gamma_{\mathrm{r}_{3}}
. In the general formulation, we have carefully avoided introducing spurious poles by directly casting our expressions in terms of the Fourier transform of the flow polytope. Had we been less careful in the derivation in sect.
4.4
, where we introduced one helper variable
z_{\textrm{r}}
for each route and, instead, introduced one variable
z_{ij}
for each difference
x_{i}^{0}-x_{f}^{0}
, we would have obtained an expression with the following oscillating part for the reduced S-matrix element,

s_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\propto\int_{\Delta^{\prime}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,f)\in V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{in}}\times V^{\textrm{ext}}_{\textrm{out}}\\
|\Gamma_{if}^{\text{ext}}|\neq 0\end{subarray}}\mathrm{d}E_{if}\sum_{\mathrm{r}\in\Gamma_{if}^{\text{ext}}}\frac{e^{i\gamma_{\mathrm{r}}E_{if}}}{\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathrm{r}^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{if}^{\text{ext}}\\
\mathrm{r}^{\prime}\neq\mathrm{r}\end{subarray}}(\gamma_{\mathrm{r}}-\gamma_{\mathrm{r}^{\prime}})},

(127)

where
\Delta^{\prime}
is a polytope similar to the flow polytope and
\Gamma_{if}
is the set of routes connecting the external vertices
i
and
f
. This alternative expression for the oscillating part features many spurious singularities and a complicated pattern of cancellations for them. For the example of eq. (
125
), it is possible to make the cancellation of spurious singularities manifest by using the Hermite-Genocchi representation of divided differences

\oint\mathrm{d}z\frac{f(z)}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}(z-a_{i})}=2\pi i\int_{\Sigma_{n}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{r}f^{(n-1)}(\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{a})\,,

(128)

which is expressed in terms of an integral over the standard simplex. The right hand side of this expression is obviously bounded. The simplex accommodates the simplest cancellation pattern of spurious singularities. More complicated patterns can be constructed by looking at generic polytopes. As an example of such cancellation patterns, let us consider the closed form expression for the Fourier transform of a simple
d
-dimensional polytope
\Delta

polytope_fourier
:

\int_{\Delta}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{r}\,e^{i\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\mathbf{r}}=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{d}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{v}\text{ vertex }\\
\text{of }\Delta\end{subarray}}\frac{e^{i\mathbf{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}}\,|\text{det}M_{v}|}{\prod_{j=1}^{d}\boldsymbol{w}_{j}(\mathbf{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}}\,,

(129)

where
\mathbf{w}_{k}(\mathbf{v})
is the
k
-th edge vector emanating from the vertex
\mathbf{v}
(since the polytope is simple, there are exactly
d
edge vectors emanating from each vertex), not necessarily normalised, and
M_{v}
is the matrix containing all such vectors as columns. Much like the right-hand side of eq. (
125
), the right-hand side of eq. (
129
) exhibits spurious cancellation of singularities: if, for a summand corresponding to the vertex
\mathbf{v}
, the denominator
\boldsymbol{w}_{j}(\mathbf{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}
vanishes, and if
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
is the vertex that the edge vector
\boldsymbol{w}_{j}(\mathbf{v})
points to, then the summand corresponding to the vertex
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
also contains the same singular denominator and the sum of the two terms is finite. Such complicated considerations are, however, completely absent when one looks at the left-hand side of eq. (
129
), which is manifestly bounded. In this way, mathematical identities that relate quantities with spurious poles to integrals over polytopes prove to be a powerful theoretical tool.

5
IR singularities in the
p
-
x
representation

Having found a representation for the S-matrix as an integral over the spatial coordinates, we can now investigate where the infrared singularities of the S-matrix are located in the space of integration variables. This will yield a precise coordinate space equivalent of infrared singularities. We will start in sect.
5.1
by deriving the collinear singularities for the triangle diagram, motivated by the intuition that collinear singularities in momentum space should correspond to long distance singularities in coordinate space that also embed a notion of collinearity. We then derive a diagram-level factorization formula for the leading behaviour in the singular limit. This is then generalised in sect.
5.2
to arbitrary graphs. In sect.
5.3
we discuss the soft singularity of the triangle diagram.

5.1
Collinear singularity on the triangle digraph

We begin with
a description of collinear singularities of the triangle diagram in the hybrid formalism. Following the intuition provided by momentum space, we expect the collinear singularity to appear when the length of the two edges adjacent to an external vertex becomes infinite. Furthermore, we expect that such distance vectors become collinear to
p_{2}
in this limit. Indeed, we find a collinear singularity if the coordinate
\vec{y}_{2}
goes to infinity
in the direction
of
\vec{p}_{2}
:

\lxSVG@closescope {{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}} }

(130)

Note that both edges of the graph that have infinite length in this limit have energy flowing into the vertex
y_{2}
. This is a necessary condition for the non-integrability of this singularity in the FOPT representation.
The total energy flowing through both edges must be equal to the energy flowing out of the
y_{2}
vertex by
energy conservation. This observation will play a role in the factorization of the integral in the soft and collinear limits.

A useful parameterization for the collinear limit is

\displaystyle\vec{y}_{2}=\lambda\vec{p}_{2}/|\vec{p}_{2}|^{2}+\sqrt{|\lambda|}\vec{y}^{\perp}_{2},\quad\vec{y}_{3}=\vec{y}_{3}\,,

(131)

where the variable
\lambda
that measures the distance in the direction
\vec{p}_{2}
and the perpendicular
part of
\vec{y}_{2}
is parameterized by
\vec{y}^{\perp}_{2}
.
We find from eq. (
118
),

\displaystyle s_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{1}\},\{p_{2},p_{3}\})

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\textrm{d}\lambda\,s_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\lambda;\{p_{1}\},\{p_{2},p_{3}\})\,,

(132)

\displaystyle s_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\lambda;\{p_{1}\},\{p_{2},p_{3}\})

\displaystyle=\frac{|\lambda|}{|\vec{p}_{2}|}e^{-i\lambda}\int\frac{\left[\textrm{d}^{2}\vec{y}^{\perp}_{2}\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{3}\right]\left[e^{-i\vec{y}_{3}\cdot\vec{p}_{3}}\right]}{8|\vec{z}_{4}||\vec{z}_{5}||\vec{z}_{6}|}\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\textrm{t}}+i\varepsilon\boldsymbol{1})\Big|_{\vec{y}_{1}=0}\,,

(133)

where we isolated the integration over
\lambda
.
Let us study the behaviour of the expression under the
\lambda
integration in the
\lambda\rightarrow\infty
limit. We remark that the
\lambda\rightarrow-\infty
limit could also be considered, but
it does not give a singular contribution. The reason for this is that the oscillating exponent
-i\lambda
in eq. (
133
) is not canceled and retains its dampening role for the integration.
We have

\displaystyle|\vec{z}_{4}|

\displaystyle=|\vec{y}_{2}-\vec{y}_{1}|=|\vec{y}_{2}|=\sqrt{\lambda^{2}/{|\vec{p}_{2}|^{2}}+\lambda(\vec{y}^{\perp}_{2})^{2}}=\frac{\lambda}{|\vec{p}_{2}|}+\frac{1}{2}|\vec{p}_{2}|(\vec{y}^{\perp}_{2})^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-1})\,,

\displaystyle|\vec{z}_{5}|

\displaystyle=|\vec{y}_{3}|\,,

(134)

\displaystyle|\vec{z}_{6}|

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{|\vec{p}_{2}|}+\frac{1}{2}|\vec{p}_{2}|(\vec{y}^{\perp}_{2})^{2}-\frac{\vec{p}_{2}\cdot\vec{y}_{3}}{|\vec{p}_{2}|}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}})\,,

and hence

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\textrm{t}}

\displaystyle=E_{1}\gamma^{\textrm{t}}_{1}+E_{2}\gamma^{\textrm{t}}_{2}+E_{3}\gamma^{\textrm{t}}_{3}=E_{1}|\vec{z}_{4}|+E_{2}|\vec{z}_{5}|+E_{3}(|\vec{z}_{5}|+|\vec{z}_{6}|)

\displaystyle=(E_{1}+E_{3})\frac{\lambda}{|\vec{p}_{2}|}+\frac{1}{2}(E_{1}+E_{3})|\vec{p}_{2}|(\vec{y}^{\perp}_{2})^{2}+(E_{2}+E_{3})|\vec{y}_{3}|-E_{3}\frac{\vec{p}_{2}\cdot\vec{y}_{3}}{|\vec{p}_{2}|}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle=-p_{2}^{0}\frac{\lambda}{|\vec{p}_{2}|}-\frac{1}{2}p_{2}^{0}|\vec{p}_{2}|(\vec{y}^{\perp}_{2})^{2}+(-p_{3}^{0}+E_{3})|\vec{y}_{3}|-E_{3}\frac{\vec{p}_{2}\cdot\vec{y}_{3}}{|\vec{p}_{2}|}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}})\,,

(135)

where we applied the constraints from the flow polytope description in eq. (
115
)
in the second line. Note that due to the total amount of energy flowing through the two infinite-length
lines being equal to
-p_{2}^{0}
, the leading term in
\lambda
does not have any dependence on the coordinates
in the flow polytope. This is a general feature which always occurs if a subset of vertices, which are only connected to the rest of the graph via two edges, are sent to
spatial infinity in the way described above.

Substitution into the
\lambda
dependent integrand

s_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\lambda;\{p_{1}\},\{p_{2},p_{3}\})

gives

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}s_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\lambda;\{p_{1}\},\{p_{2},p_{3}\})=\frac{|\vec{p}_{2}|}{4\lambda}e^{-i\lambda-ip_{2}^{0}(\frac{\lambda}{|\vec{p}_{2}|}+i\varepsilon)}\int\textrm{d}^{2}\vec{y}^{\perp}_{2}e^{-\frac{1}{2}ip_{2}^{0}(|\vec{p}_{2}|(\vec{y}^{\perp}_{2})^{2}+i\varepsilon)}\\
\times\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{3}e^{-ip_{3}^{0}|\vec{y}_{3}|-i\vec{y}_{3}\cdot\vec{p}_{3}}}{2|\vec{y}_{3}|}\int_{\mathcal{F}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}}\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{E}e^{iE_{3}\left(|\vec{y}_{3}|-\frac{\vec{p}_{2}\cdot\vec{y}_{3}}{|\vec{p}_{2}|}\right)}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}}).\end{gathered}

Let us examine this integrand in the limit
p_{2}^{2}\rightarrow 0
.
Recall that
p_{2}^{0}<0
. Hence, in this limit

-p_{2}^{0}\rightarrow|\vec{p}_{2}|
.
The first term involving
\lambda
leads to a
logarithmically divergent integrand
from the
\lambda\rightarrow\infty
part of the integration.
The oscillatory part vanishes if we set
p_{2}^{0}=-|\vec{p}_{2}|

and we lose the dampening of the divergence due to the oscillation.
Explicitly, the divergent part in the on-shell limit is produced by the exponential integral,

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}\textrm{d}\lambda\frac{e^{i\left(-\frac{p_{2}^{0}}{|\vec{p}_{2}|}-1\right)\lambda}}{\lambda}.

(138)

Changing the lower boundary if this integral only results in a finite change of the integral in the singular limit

-p_{2}^{0}\rightarrow|\vec{p}_{2}|
. By standard asymptotic expansions
abramowitz1964handbook
of the exponential integral we find

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}\textrm{d}\lambda\frac{e^{i\left(-\frac{p_{2}^{0}}{|\vec{p}_{2}|}-1\right)\lambda}}{\lambda}

\displaystyle=-\log\left(-\frac{p_{2}^{0}}{|\vec{p}_{2}|}-1\right)+(\text{terms finite when $p_{2}^{2}\rightarrow 0$})

\displaystyle=-\log\left(\frac{p^{2}_{2}}{p_{1}^{2}}\right)+(\text{terms finite when $p_{2}^{2}\rightarrow 0$})\,,

(139)

where we normalized the argument of the logarithm using
p_{1}^{2}
.
The remaining part of the integral in eq. (
132
) remains finite in the

p_{2}^{2}\rightarrow 0
limit.

In eq. (
5.1
) we find a Gaussian integral over the
perpendicular contribution in the collinear limit.
We may interpret this Gaussian as the
interaction event at
\vec{y}_{2}
being localized in a cylinder of
radius
1/|p_{2}^{0}|
around the collinear direction.
The precise distribution is given by an imaginary Gaussian
distribution with standard deviation given by this radius. The total contribution from the perpendicular part reads,
after using
p_{2}^{0}=-|\vec{p}_{2}|
,

\displaystyle\int\textrm{d}^{2}\vec{y}^{\perp}_{2}e^{\frac{1}{2}i((p_{2}^{0})^{2}(\vec{y}^{\perp}_{2})^{2}+i\varepsilon)}=\frac{2\pi i}{(p_{2}^{0})^{2}}\,.

(140)

Using the evaluation of these two integrals in eqs. (
5.1
) and (
140
), and dropping terms that vanish in the limit
p^{2}_{2}\rightarrow 0
, the reduced S-matrix in eq. (
5.1
) takes the form

\displaystyle s_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{1}\},\{p_{2},p_{3}\})=-\frac{2\pi i}{4|\vec{p}_{2}|}\log\left(\frac{p_{2}^{2}}{p_{1}^{2}}\right)\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{3}e^{-ip_{3}^{0}|\vec{y}_{3}|-i\vec{y}_{3}\cdot\vec{p}_{3}}}{2|\vec{y}_{3}|}

\displaystyle\times\int_{\mathcal{F}_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}}\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{E}e^{iE_{3}\left(|\vec{y}_{3}|-\frac{\vec{p}_{2}\cdot\vec{y}_{3}}{|\vec{p}_{2}|}\right)}+(\text{terms finite when $p_{2}^{2}\rightarrow 0$}).

(141)

We can recast this expression in a more illuminating form. Since dependence on both
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
dropped out of the Fourier transform, we can integrate over them, changing variables to
x=E_{3}/p_{2}^{0}
for the remaining integration. The variable
x
parameterizes the proportion of energy that flows through the lower infinite line in (
130
) in the collinear limit. As a result we obtain

\displaystyle s_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{1}\},\{p_{2},p_{3}\})=-\frac{2\pi i}{4}\log\left(\frac{p_{2}^{2}}{p_{1}^{2}}\right)\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}x\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{3}e^{-ip_{3}^{0}|\vec{y}_{3}|+ix|\vec{y}_{3}||\vec{p}_{2}|}}{2|\vec{y}_{3}|}e^{-i\vec{y}_{3}\cdot\vec{p}_{3}-ix\vec{p}_{2}\cdot\vec{y}_{3}}

\displaystyle+(\text{terms finite when $p_{2}^{2}\rightarrow 0$}).

(142)

The integral over
\vec{y}_{3}
can be identified with an integral over the
reduced S-matrix element of the
hard graph

(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})_{\text{hard}}
. More precisely, consider the diagram

y_{1}

y_{3}

p_{1}

p_{3}

(1-x)p_{2}

xp_{2}

(143)

which is the lower left part of the triangle diagram
in eq. (
130
), which stays
hard
, i.e. bounded, in the singular limit.
The S-matrix element corresponding to the hard graph is, by similar reasoning as above,

\displaystyle s_{(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})_{\text{hard}}}(\{p_{1}\},\{(1-x)p_{2},xp_{2},p_{3}\})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{3}e^{-i\vec{y}_{3}\cdot\vec{p}_{3}-ix\vec{y}_{3}\cdot\vec{p}_{2}}}{2|\vec{y}_{3}|}\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})_{\text{hard}}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\textrm{t}}+i\varepsilon\boldsymbol{1})\Big|_{\vec{y}_{1}=0}\,.

(144)

The diagram allows for three paths, starting at the incoming leg labelled by
p_{1}
and ending up at the external legs labelled by
(1-x)p_{2}
,
xp_{2}
and
p_{3}
. The paths have length
0
,
|\vec{y}_{3}|
and
|\vec{y}_{3}|
, respectively (recall that for the reduced S-matrix, we have shifted
\vec{y}_{1}
to be located at the origin). Evaluating the Fourier transform of the corresponding flow polytope gives

\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})_{\text{hard}}}^{\{p^{0}_{a}\}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\textrm{t}}+i\varepsilon\boldsymbol{1})\Big|_{\vec{y}_{1}=0}=\exp\left(-i(xp_{2}^{0}+p_{3}^{0})|\vec{y}_{3}|\right)\,,

(145)

where the reduced flow polytope is just a single point given by

E_{2}=p_{1}^{0}+(1-x)p_{2}^{0}=-p_{3}^{0}-xp_{2}^{0}
. We labeled both the energy and the relevant coordinates in accordance with the labeling of the full digraph
(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})
. Hence,

\displaystyle s_{(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})_{\text{hard}}}(\{p_{1}\},\{(1-x)p_{2},xp_{2},p_{3}\})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{3}e^{-i\vec{y}_{3}\cdot\vec{p}_{3}-ix\vec{y}_{3}\cdot\vec{p}_{2}}}{2|\vec{y}_{3}|}e^{-i(p_{3}^{0}+xp_{2}^{0})(|\vec{y}_{3}|+i\varepsilon)}\,.

(146)

It follows by substitution of eq. (
146
) into (
5.1
) that

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}s_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{1}\},\{p_{2},p_{3}\})=-\frac{2\pi i}{4}\log\frac{p_{2}^{2}}{p_{1}^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\textrm{d}x\,s_{(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})_{\text{hard}}}(\{p_{1}\},\{(1-x)p_{2},xp_{2},p_{3}\})+\mathcal{O}_{p_{2}^{2}\rightarrow 0}(1)\,.\end{gathered}

We hence resolved the collinear singularity
p_{2}^{2}\rightarrow 0
of the oriented triangle
explicitly. This simple example shows that the leading singular part of the S-matrix in the collinear limit under inspection can be expressed in a factorised manner. We can actually evaluate the hard S-matrix explicitly

s_{(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})_{\text{hard}}}(\{p_{1}\},\{(1-x)p_{2},xp_{2},p_{3}\})=\frac{-\pi}{(p_{3}^{0}+xp_{2}^{0})^{2}-|\vec{p}_{3}+x\vec{p}_{2}|^{2}}\,.

(148)

For massless on-shell, non-exceptional, values of
p_{2}
and
p_{3}
the S-matrix is only singular when
x\rightarrow 0
, with a logarithmic divergence. In sect.
5.3
we will see that
x\rightarrow 0
corresponds to the soft singularity.
We can now generalize the analysis of collinear singularities to more complicated integrals.

We emphasise that the collinear singularity required the two collinear edges
e_{4},e_{6}
to have the same flow direction. If, for instance, we would have considered the limit where
\vec{y}_{3}
is sent to infinity in the direction of
\vec{p}_{3}
instead, then we would not have encountered a singularity.
The reason is that the Fourier transform of the flow polytope does not factor in this case and the integral remains finite due to the rapid oscillation of the Fourier transform.

5.2
General collinear singularities in the
p
-
x
S-matrix representation

For a general FOPT graph, a collinear singularity is present whenever there exists a two-edge cut that divides the graph into two separate, connected components, such that each component has at least one in- or out-going momentum. Furthermore, the two edges being cut are required to
have the same orientation
. More precisely, suppose we are interested in

s_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\},\{p_{k+1},\ldots,p_{n}\})
,
the reduced S-matrix element
as defined in (
4.4
), associated to the digraph
({G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}})
. Then, consider the following bipartition into components
(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})_{\text{col}}
and
(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})_{\text{hard}}
,

(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})_{\text{hard}}
(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})_{\text{col}}
\cdots
\cdots
p_{1}
p_{2}
p_{k}
p_{k+1}
p_{n-1}
p_{n}

(149)

which features an admissible two-cut, indicated by the orange line. For non-exceptional external momenta, the collinear singularity is attained when
(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})_{\text{col}}
contains only one external leg (in- or out-going), as in the picture above.
As illustrated in the last section, the collinear singularity appears when the vertices of the
(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})_{\text{col}}
part of the graph move to infinity in the direction of
\vec{p}_{n}
.
By energy conservation, the total energy flow through the cut is equal to the value of
p_{n}^{0}
.

Elementary power counting along the lines of the previous section shows that only two-cuts yield non-integrable
collinear singularities. Three- and
n
-cuts are integrable in the
single
(i.e. when no multiple overlapping collinear singularities are considered) collinear limit: to see this, observe that all the inverse distances associated with the cut edges lead to a cumulative scaling of
-n
, while the measure will scale the same as that for the parameterization of eq. (
131
), i.e. like two powers, independently of
n
, and consistently with the fact that we are considering a single limit. This gives an overall scaling of
2-n
, from which the power-counting argument follows.

In the limit where the split momenta goes on-shell, in our case
p_{n}^{2}\rightarrow 0
, we have the diagrammatic factorization law:

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}s_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\},\{p_{k+1},\ldots,p_{n}\})=-\frac{2\pi i}{4}\log\frac{p_{n}^{2}}{Q^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\textrm{d}x\,s_{(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})_{\text{hard}}}s_{(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})_{\text{col}}}+\mathcal{O}_{p_{n}^{2}\rightarrow 0}(1)\,,\end{gathered}

where
Q
is an arbitrary external momentum scale (for example,
Q=p_{1}+...+p_{k}
) and
s_{(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})_{\text{hard}}}
and
s_{(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})_{\text{col}}}
are the reduced S-matrix elements associated to the subgraphs, i.e.

(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})_{\text{hard}}
\cdots
\cdots
p_{1}
p_{2}
p_{k}
p_{k+1}
p_{n-1}
(1-x)p_{n}
xp_{n}

and

(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})_{\text{col}}
-xp_{n}
-(1-x)p_{n}
p_{n}

(151)

with the dependence on the external momenta as indicated.
Note that in the previous example the contribution from the collinear part of the diagram was
trivial as it just consisted of a single vertex.

5.3
Soft-collinear singularity of the triangle diagram

We will illustrate the appearance of soft singularities in the triangle diagram of eq. (
114
).
This soft singularity will be a
nested singularity
,
in the sense that it is contained in the previous collinear limit.

By eqs. (
146
) and (
5.1
) we have the following expression for the S-matrix element in the collinear limit:

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}s_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{1}\},\{p_{2},p_{3}\})=\\
-\frac{2\pi i}{4}\log\frac{p_{2}^{2}}{p_{1}^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\textrm{d}x\,\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{3}e^{-i\vec{y}_{3}\cdot\vec{p}_{3}-ix\vec{y}_{3}\cdot\vec{p}_{2}}}{2|\vec{y}_{3}|}e^{i(-p_{3}^{0}-xp_{2}^{0})(|\vec{y}_{3}|+i\varepsilon)}+\mathcal{O}_{p_{2}^{2}\rightarrow 0}(1)\,.\end{gathered}

The soft singularity is associated with the limit in which, in addition to the vertex
\vec{y}_{2}
, the vertex
\vec{y}_{3}
in the diagram below is sent to infinity along the direction
\vec{p}_{3}
as indicated.

y_{1}

y_{2}

y_{3}

p_{1}

p_{2}

p_{3}

(154)

The soft singularity only appears if both
p_{2},p_{3}
are on-shell, i.e.
p_{2}^{2},p_{3}^{2}\rightarrow 0
.
The large distance limit can be parameterized as follows in the integrand of eq. (
5.3
)

\displaystyle\vec{y}_{3}=\lambda{\vec{p}_{3}}/{|\vec{p}_{3}|^{2}}+\sqrt{|\lambda|}\vec{y}^{\perp}_{3}\,,\;\;\;\;x=\frac{1}{|\lambda|}x^{\prime}\,,

(155)

where, considering
\lambda\rightarrow\infty
, we send
\vec{y}_{3}
to infinity and let the energy flow through the bottom right edge in (
154
), which is parameterized by the energy fraction parameter
x
, go to zero.
That means this edge is
becoming soft
.
In the limit
\lambda\rightarrow\infty
we find

\displaystyle|\vec{z}_{5}|

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{|\vec{p}_{3}|}+\frac{1}{2}|\vec{p}_{3}|(\vec{y}^{\perp}_{3})^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-1}).

(156)

Applying this to the relevant part of eq. (
5.3
), we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}s_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{1}\},\{p_{2},p_{3}\})=-\frac{2\pi i}{8}\log\frac{p_{2}^{2}}{p_{1}^{2}}\\
\times\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\textrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda}\left(\int_{0}^{\lambda}\textrm{d}x^{\prime}\,\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\vec{y}^{\perp}_{3}e^{-i\lambda-ix^{\prime}\frac{\vec{p}_{3}\cdot\vec{p}_{2}}{\vec{p}_{3}^{2}}-i\frac{p_{3}^{0}}{|\vec{p}_{3}|}\lambda-i\frac{1}{2}p_{3}^{0}|\vec{p}_{3}|(\vec{y}^{\perp}_{3})^{2}-ix^{\prime}(\frac{p_{2}^{0}}{|\vec{p}_{3}|}-i\varepsilon)}+\mathcal{O}_{\lambda\rightarrow\infty}(\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}}),\right)\\
+\mathcal{O}_{p_{2}^{2}\rightarrow 0}(1)\,.\end{gathered}

In the
p_{3}^{2}\rightarrow 0
limit, the integration over
x^{\prime}
results in,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\lambda}\textrm{d}x^{\prime}\,e^{-ix^{\prime}\left(\frac{\vec{p}_{3}\cdot\vec{p}_{2}}{\vec{p}_{3}^{2}}+\frac{p_{2}^{0}}{|\vec{p}_{3}|}-i\varepsilon\right)}=\frac{i(p^{0}_{3})^{2}}{p^{0}_{2}p^{0}_{3}-\vec{p}_{2}\cdot\vec{p}_{3}}+\mathcal{O}_{\lambda\rightarrow\infty}(e^{-\varepsilon\lambda}).

(160)

The other parts of the integral have already been evaluated in the last section and we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}s_{G,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\{p_{1}\},\{p_{2},p_{3}\})=-i\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{8}\frac{\log\frac{p_{2}^{2}}{p_{1}^{2}}\log\frac{p_{3}^{2}}{p_{1}^{2}}}{p_{2}\cdot p_{3}}+\mathcal{O}_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{2}^{2}\rightarrow 0\\
p_{3}^{2}\rightarrow 0\end{subarray}}(1).\end{gathered}

This expression gives the full contribution to the
reduced S-matrix element of the oriented triangle diagram (
114
) in the limit
p_{2}^{2},p_{3}^{2}\rightarrow 0
. We find the expected double-
\log
Sudakov structure. The expression stays well-defined as long as
p_{2}\cdot p_{3}=p^{0}_{2}p^{0}_{3}-\vec{p}_{2}\cdot\vec{p}_{3}\neq 0
. This is expected as
p_{1}^{2}=(p_{2}+p_{3})^{2}=2p_{2}\cdot p_{3}
is required to be non-vanishing by energy conservation.
Similar soft-factorization phenomena can be observed for the
p
-
x
S-matrix representations of other FOPT diagrams. We will leave the detailed analysis of general soft singularities to a future work.

6
Conclusion

In this paper we introduced a new version of diagram-based perturbation theory in quantum field theory: flow-oriented perturbation theory. Instead of the usual covariant momentum space perturbation theory in four-dimensional Minkowski space, it is effectively a three-dimensional coordinate space integral representation in which the time integrations are resolved via the residue theorem. This representation is dual to time-ordered perturbation theory. The main features of this new representation are that it is combinatorial and canonical, and that the
i\varepsilon
dependence simplified. Our derivation of the canonical FOPT Feynman rules in sect.
2
, which associate a unique integral expression to each FOPT diagram, is illustrated by detailed examples.

We described UV singularities within the FOPT formalism and showed that it yields equivalent power-counting to the covariant picture. Interestingly, UV and IR singularities turn out to be more isolated within FOPT than in the covariant framework. It would be interesting to study this phenomenon in more detail by analyzing the nested structure of FOPT divergences e.g. by using Hopf algebra or lattice techniques
Connes:1999yr
;
Figueroa:2004hb
;
Borinsky:2014xwa
;
Beekveldt:2020kzk
.

Our derivation relied on the underlying QFT being formulated in four-dimensional Minkowski spacetime. Especially, while treating gauge theories it is desirable to be able to use dimensional regularization, i.e. to work in
4-2\epsilon
dimensions. We leave the formulation of dimensionally regularized FOPT to a future work. An immediate alternative to dimensional regularization, which removes UV singularities and which can be used in conjunction with FOPT is a BHPZ subtraction scheme as it has been applied for three-dimensional momentum-space representations in
Capatti:2022tit
.

The FOPT representation also features finite distance singularities whose structure we discussed in sect.
3
, where we also compared them to analyses put forward in
Sterman3
;
StermanErdogan
;
ErdoganCS
. We concluded that these singularities are somewhat different from the usual collinear and soft IR divergences that are observed in momentum space.

This observation led us to derive a new representation for the perturbative expansion of the S-matrix in sect.
4
. This
p
-
x
S-matrix representation is of hybrid momentum and coordinate space nature, as external parameters are given in terms of momenta, but internal integrations are performed in coordinate space. This representation produces an effectively three-dimensional coordinate space integral, which is modulated by the Fourier transform of the
flow polytope
.
The flow polytope has many remarkable properties
gallo1978extreme
;
stanley2000acyclic
;
baldoni2004counting
;
meszaros2015flow
;
meszaros2017polytope
;
benedetti2019combinatorial
.
We exposed the explicit resolution of spurious singularities within this Fourier transform. We provided some general remarks on such resolutions in sect.
4.7
.

As expected from the covariant picture, the
p
-
x
S-matrix representation features soft and collinear divergences. We illustrated their appearance in sect.
5
and showed the central role of the flow polytope and its Fourier transform. Moreover, we explained how manifest collinear factorization at the diagram level is attained in the
p
-
x
S-matrix representation. Even though we also illustrate how soft divergences appear, we postpone the derivation of a general per-diagram factorization law for soft singularities to future work.

It would be interesting to study these singular structures with respect to their nesting e.g. as in ref.
Borinsky:2015mga
, and it might be possible to design a new IR subtraction scheme along the lines of ref.
Herzog:2018ily
;
Ma:2019hjq
using the
p
-
x
S-matrix representation. As we illustrated in sect.
5
, these singularities are naturally regularized with the value of the external kinematics and computations in singular limits are comparatively easy.

As we show in the appendix, an interesting application of the FOPT representation in the context of explicit computations are phase space unitarity-cut integrals. The FOPT representation applied to these, turns out to have the same dimension for all cut diagrams in contrast to covariant momentum space, where the integration measure effectively has a different dimension for each cut diagram. Over all cut diagrams we expect cancellation of IR divergences by the KLN theorem and within the FOPT representation this cancellation should be local. In appendix
A
, we illustrate the application of the FOPT approach to unitarity-cut integrals and summarize the associated Feynman rules, but also here we leave the exploration of potential local cancellation properties with possible applications for numerical evaluations of cross-sections to a future work.

MB was supported by Dr. Max Rössler, the Walter Haefner Foundation and the ETH Zürich Foundation. ZC would like to thank Babis Anastasiou for his continuous support. ASB and EL thank the Institute for Theoretical Studies at the ETH Zürich for hospitality during the completion of this work. ASB received financial support from Spanish MICINN PID2019-108655GB-I00/AEI/10.13039/501100011033 grant and expresses their gratitude to Juan Ferrera for useful discussions at the early stages of this research.

Appendix A
Unitarity, cut integrals and Cutkosky’s Theorem

The FOPT representation that we derived in sect.
2
takes an interesting form for unitarity-cut based phase space integrals. In this appendix, we discuss this viewpoint along the lines of a classic exposition of phase space integrals focused on the largest time equation
Veltman2
. The result is also related to Cutkosky’s theorem
Cutkosky:1960sp
. The following application of the Cutkosky rules to the FOPT representation brings us close to results on the general analyticity properties of Feynman integrals, as described in
Bloch:2015efx
;
Berghoff:2020bug
;
Berghoff:2022vah
.
This FOPT-cut representation has some remarkable properties in this regard. For instance the integrals associated to virtual and real corrections turn out to have the same dimension, so that both can be put on the same measure. Hence, the following considerations might be useful to pursue explicit computations of phase space integrals with manifest cancellation of real and virtual singularities.

Here, we briefly present this FOPT-based representation of cut integrals.
Given a subset of vertices
V\subset V_{G}
of a graph
G
, the
subgraph induced
from
V
is the subgraph of
G
that contains all edges whose both end-vertices lie in
V
. A
cut

\mathfrak{C}
of a Feynman graph
\Gamma
is partition of the vertices
V_{G}
into two parts
V_{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faSunO}}},V_{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faMoonO}}}
such that the respective induced subgraphs with edges
E_{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faSunO}}},E_{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faMoonO}}}
from both parts are connected and each contains at least one external vertex. The edges that have one end in
V_{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faSunO}}}
and one in
V_{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faMoonO}}}
are
cut edges
,
E_{\mathfrak{C}}
. Intuitively, we think of energy flowing from the

-side to the

-side.

Recall that the
cut propagator
is given by

\displaystyle\Delta^{\pm}(z)=\int\frac{\textrm{d}^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}2\pi\delta(p^{2})\theta(\pm p_{0})e^{-ip\cdot z}=-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{(z^{0}\mp i\varepsilon)^{2}-|\vec{z}\,|^{2}}\right).

It follows, from an argument due to Veltman
Veltman2
, that the real part
5
5

5

Due to an extra conventional factor of
i
this real part of the integral contributes to the
imaginary
part of the Feynman amplitude.
of a Feynman integral can be expressed in terms of a sum over
cut integrals
(see
(
Martinus
, Ch. 8)
or
(
le1991quantum
, Ch. 10.4)
for a detailed derivation):

\displaystyle A_{G}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{|V^{\textrm{ext}}|})+A_{G}^{*}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{|V^{\textrm{ext}}|})=-\sum_{\text{cuts }\mathfrak{C}\text{ of }G}A_{G,\mathfrak{C}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{|V^{\textrm{ext}}|})\,,

(162)

where the cut integrals
A_{G,\mathfrak{C}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{|V^{\textrm{ext}}|})
are given by the expression

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}A_{G,\mathfrak{C}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{|V^{\textrm{ext}}|})=\\
=(-ig)^{|V^{\textrm{int}}_{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faSunO}}}|}(ig)^{|V^{\textrm{int}}_{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faMoonO}}}|}\left(\prod_{v\in V^{\textrm{int}}}\int\textrm{d}^{4}y_{v}\right)\left(\prod_{e\in E_{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faSunO}}}}\Delta_{F}(z_{e})\right)\left(\prod_{e\in E_{\mathfrak{C}}}\Delta^{+}(z_{e})\right)\left(\prod_{e\in E_{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faMoonO}}}}\Delta^{*}_{F}(z_{e})\right).\end{gathered}

In contrast to the original Feynman integral, the propagators on the cut are replaced with the positive frequency
\Delta^{+}
cut propagator and the Feynman propagators on the

-side of the cut are replaced with the complex conjugate Feynman propagator.

We have the following expressions for the Fourier transform of the coordinate space propagator

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\textrm{d}z^{0}_{e}e^{iE_{e}z^{0}_{e}}\Delta^{+}(z_{e})=-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\textrm{d}z^{0}_{e}\frac{e^{iE_{e}z^{0}_{e}}}{(z^{0}_{e}-i\varepsilon)^{2}-\vec{z}_{e}^{2}}\\
=-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\textrm{d}z^{0}_{e}\frac{e^{iE_{e}z^{0}_{e}}}{2|\vec{z}_{e}|}\left(\frac{1}{z^{0}_{e}-|\vec{z}_{e}|-i\varepsilon}-\frac{1}{z^{0}_{e}+|\vec{z}_{e}|-i\varepsilon}\right)\\
=-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{2\pi i}{2|\vec{z}_{e}|}\theta(E_{e})\left(e^{iE_{e}(|\vec{z}_{e}|+i\varepsilon)}-e^{iE_{e}(-|\vec{z}_{e}|+i\varepsilon)}\right)\,,\end{gathered}

with the analogous Cauchy integrals

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\textrm{d}z^{0}_{e}e^{iE_{e}z^{0}_{e}}\Delta_{F}(z_{e})&=-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{2\pi i}{2|\vec{z}_{e}|}\left(\theta(E_{e})e^{iE_{e}(|\vec{z}_{e}|+i\varepsilon)}+\theta(-E_{e})e^{-iE_{e}(|\vec{z}_{e}|+i\varepsilon)}\right)\,,\\
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\textrm{d}z^{0}_{e}e^{iE_{e}z^{0}_{e}}\Delta_{F}^{*}(z_{e})&=-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{-2\pi i}{2|\vec{z}_{e}|}\left(\theta(E_{e})e^{iE_{e}(-|\vec{z}_{e}|+i\varepsilon)}+\theta(-E_{e})e^{-iE_{e}(-|\vec{z}_{e}|+i\varepsilon)}\right)\,.\end{aligned}

(168)

Repeating the derivation in sect.
2.3
, now for the cut integral in eq. (
A
), while using the Fourier transforms of the respective propagators, results in a representation of a cut integral as a sum over FOPT-cut integrals.

FOPT Feynman rules for cut integrals

The result is the following set of FOPT-cut integral Feynman rules for a digraph
(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})
with a cut
\mathfrak{C}
.

1.

The integral is
0
if the closed directed graph
(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})^{\circ}
is not strongly connected or if the admissible paths on the cut do not go from the

-side to the

-side of the graph.

2.

Multiply a factor of
-ig
(
ig
) for each

-side (

-side) interaction vertex.

3.

For each internal vertex
v\in V^{\text{int}}
of the digraph
(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})
integrate over
3
-dimensional space with the measure
2\pi\int\textrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{v}
.

4.

For each edge
e
of the graph multiply a factor of
\frac{\mp i}{8\pi^{2}|\vec{z}_{e}|}
with a
-
sign for a

-side or a cut edge, and a
+
sign for a

-side edge.

5.

For each entirely uncut directed admissible path,
\textrm{p}_{\ell}
, of
(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})^{\circ}
multiply a factor of

\displaystyle\frac{i}{\sum_{e\in\textrm{p}_{\ell}}|\vec{z}_{e}|+\tau_{\mathcal{\textrm{p}}_{\ell}}+i\varepsilon}

if
\textrm{p}_{\ell}
consists entirely of

-side edges

\displaystyle\frac{i}{-\sum_{e\in\textrm{p}_{\ell}}|\vec{z}_{e}|+\tau_{\textrm{p}_{\ell}}+i\varepsilon}

if
\textrm{p}_{\ell}
consists entirely of

-side edges

where the sum in the denominator goes over all edges that are in the admissible path
\textrm{p}_{\ell}
and
\tau_{\mathcal{\textrm{p}}_{\ell}}
is the time difference that has passed while going through the
\circ
vertex, or
0
if the admissible path does not go through the
\circ
vertex, i.e. is a cycle.

6.

For each directed admissible path
\textrm{p}_{\ell}
of
(G,\boldsymbol{\sigma})^{\circ}
that passes the cut
\mathfrak{C}
, multiply a factor of

\displaystyle\frac{-2i|\vec{z}_{e_{\mathfrak{C}}}|}{\left(\sum_{e\in\textrm{p}_{\ell}^{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faSunO}}}}|\vec{z}_{e}|-\sum_{e\in\textrm{p}_{\ell}^{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faMoonO}}}}|\vec{z}_{e}|+\tau_{\textrm{p}_{\ell}}+i\varepsilon\right)^{2}-{\vec{z}_{e_{\mathfrak{C}}}}^{\,2}}\,,

where we sum over the uncut

-side and

-side edges in
\textrm{p}_{\ell}
,
\textrm{p}_{\ell}^{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faSunO}}}
and
\textrm{p}_{\ell}^{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faMoonO}}}
, and where
e_{\mathfrak{C}}
denotes the unique edge of the admissible path that is on the cut.
The edge is unique because, once the path passes over the cut edge, the energy cannot flow back through the cut.

(1a)&&\;\;\;\;\textrm{p}_{1}&\;\;\;\;\textrm{p}_{2}&\;\;\;\;\textrm{p}_{3}\end{array}

Hence, applying the FOPT-cut Feynman rules from above to the cut diagram
(1a)

results in the following expression

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}A_{(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\mathfrak{C})_{(1a)}}=-8\frac{(2\pi)^{2}g^{4}}{(8\pi^{2})^{5}}\int\frac{\textrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{1}\textrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{2}}{|\vec{z}_{1}||\vec{z}_{2}||\vec{z}_{3}||\vec{z}_{4}||\vec{z}_{5}|}\\
\times\frac{|\vec{z}_{2}|}{(-|\vec{z}_{3}|+\tau+i\varepsilon)^{2}-\vec{z}_{2}^{\,2}}\frac{|\vec{z}_{5}|}{(|\vec{z}_{1}|-|\vec{z}_{3}|+\tau+i\varepsilon)^{2}-\vec{z}_{5}^{\,2}}\frac{|\vec{z}_{4}|}{(|\vec{z}_{1}|+\tau+i\varepsilon)^{2}-\vec{z}_{4}^{\,2}}.\end{gathered}

where we accounted for the admissible paths through the cut,
23
,
153
and
14
via the appropriate denominators, and
\tau=x_{2}^{0}-x_{1}^{0}
. Analogously, applying the Feynman rules to the FOPT-cut graphs
(1b)
and
(1c)
results in

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}A_{(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\mathfrak{C})_{(1b)}}=8\frac{(2\pi)^{2}g^{4}}{(8\pi^{2})^{5}}\int\frac{\textrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{1}\textrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{2}}{|\vec{z}_{1}||\vec{z}_{2}||\vec{z}_{3}||\vec{z}_{4}||\vec{z}_{5}|}\\
\times\frac{|\vec{z}_{1}|}{(-|\vec{z}_{4}|+\tau+i\varepsilon)^{2}-\vec{z}_{1}^{\,2}}\frac{|\vec{z}_{1}|}{(-|\vec{z}_{5}|-|\vec{z}_{3}|+\tau+i\varepsilon)^{2}-\vec{z}_{1}^{\,2}}\frac{|\vec{z}_{2}|}{(-|\vec{z}_{3}|+\tau+i\varepsilon)^{2}-\vec{z}_{2}^{\,2}}\,,\end{gathered}

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}A_{(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\mathfrak{C})_{(1c)}}(x_{1},x_{2})=-8\frac{(2\pi)^{2}g^{4}}{(8\pi^{2})^{5}}\int\frac{\textrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{1}\textrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{2}}{|\vec{z}_{1}||\vec{z}_{2}||\vec{z}_{3}||\vec{z}_{4}||\vec{z}_{5}|}\\
\times\frac{|\vec{z}_{4}|}{(|\vec{z}_{1}|+\tau+i\varepsilon)^{2}-\vec{z}_{4}^{\,2}}\frac{|\vec{z}_{3}|}{(|\vec{z}_{1}|+|\vec{z}_{5}|+\tau+i\varepsilon)^{2}-\vec{z}_{3}^{\,2}}\frac{|\vec{z}_{3}|}{(|\vec{z}_{2}|+\tau+i\varepsilon)^{2}-\vec{z}_{3}^{\,2}}\,.\end{gathered}

These three FOPT-cut integrals all correspond to the same directed graph with different cuts on it. Each integrand features three factors in the denominator, which each corresponding to a unique route from the left-most to the right-most vertex.

For the other possible flow orientations of the graph, we only have two routes through the diagram and one closed cyclic energy flow:

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}A_{(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\mathfrak{C})_{(2b)}}(x_{1},x_{2})=-4\frac{(2\pi)^{2}g^{4}}{(8\pi^{2})^{5}}\int\frac{\textrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{1}\textrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{2}}{|\vec{z}_{1}||\vec{z}_{2}||\vec{z}_{3}||\vec{z}_{4}||\vec{z}_{5}|}\\
\times\frac{|\vec{z}_{1}|}{(-|\vec{z}_{5}|-|\vec{z}_{3}|+\tau+i\varepsilon)^{2}-\vec{z}_{1}^{\,2}}\frac{|\vec{z}_{2}|}{(-|\vec{z}_{3}|+\tau+i\varepsilon)^{2}-\vec{z}_{2}^{\,2}}\frac{1}{-|\vec{z}_{5}|-|\vec{z}_{3}|-|\vec{z}_{4}|}\,,\end{gathered}

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}A_{(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\mathfrak{C})_{(2c)}}(x_{1},x_{2})=4\frac{(2\pi)^{2}g^{4}}{(8\pi^{2})^{5}}\int\frac{\textrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{1}\textrm{d}^{3}\vec{y}_{2}}{|\vec{z}_{1}||\vec{z}_{2}||\vec{z}_{3}||\vec{z}_{4}||\vec{z}_{5}|}\\
\times\frac{|\vec{z}_{4}|}{(|\vec{z}_{1}|+\tau+i\varepsilon)^{2}-\vec{z}_{4}^{\,2}}\frac{|\vec{z}_{3}|}{(|\vec{z}_{1}|+|\vec{z}_{5}|+\tau+i\varepsilon)^{2}-\vec{z}_{3}^{\,2}}\frac{1}{|\vec{z}_{2}|+|\vec{z}_{5}|+|\vec{z}_{1}|}\,.\end{gathered}

The energy-flow-oriented cut graph
(2b)
has the cycle
534
and the graph
(2c)
the cycle
152
.

Even though, e.g.,
(1a)
and both
(1b)
and
(1c)
have differently sized cuts, the corresponding integrands are of the same dimension. This is a convenient situation from the perspective of the numerical evaluation of these integrals, as we can put all
(1a)
,
(1b)
and
(1c)
under the same integral sign. We expect IR singularities to cancel locally in our proposed representation, but we postpone the detailed analysis of this conjecture to a future work.
